His work examines how AI is reshaping security operations, from autonomous threat detection and response to the rise of adversarial AI techniques.

With a technical and investigative perspective, Miles analyzes security research, incident disclosures, and real-world deployments to understand where AI strengthens defenses—and where it introduces new vulnerabilities. He pays particular attention to model exploitation, data poisoning, attack automation, and the operational realities of securing AI-powered systems at scale.

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/payers/humana-athenahealth-collaborate-expanded-electronic-prior-authorizations-nationwide" hreflang="en">Humana, athenahealth collaborate on expanded electronic prior authorizations nationwide</a>

📅 2026-11-03  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →
Humana, athenahealth collaborate on expanded electronic prior authorizations nationwide" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Humana and athenahealth are expanding electronic prior authorizations nationwide in a newly announced collaboration.

Through athenaPayer, Humana is able to connect with over 180,000 providers across the athenaOne network, which the partners said will create a more connected experience between the health plan and clinicians.

“Every phone call, fax and portal log-in adds work for a practice trying to get a patient the care they need,” said Paul Brient, athenahealth chief product and operations officer, in a statement. “Prior authorization shouldn’t require care teams to navigate a different process for every health plan. By connecting health plans and practices through one solution with a common workflow, we’re creating a more scalable way to exchange information, reducing work for both providers and payers while, most importantly, helping patients access care faster.”

Athenahealth internal data show more than 6,000 providers processed nearly 35,000 electronic prior authorizations with Humana in September, with over 70% completed without requiring additional information from staff.

The collaboration reflects Humana’s broader strategy to improve care access through requiring fewer prior authorizations and making the process simpler when needed, executives said.

“Our goal is straightforward: help people get the right care at the right time while reducing unnecessary burden on the physicians and care teams who serve them,” said Traci Granston, M.D., Humana vice president of clinical innovation and trend, in a statement. “… This collaboration with athenahealth is another step forward, ensuring that providers spend less time navigating administrative processes and more time focused on patient care.”

In June 2025, insurers nationwide—including Humana—agreed to take steps to reform prior authorization, including supporting electronic prior authorizations.

Why Offerpad is getting more selective on home buys

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Offerpad said faster rising mortgage rates and affordability pressures slowed home sales in Q3 2026. The iBuyer tightened acquisition criteria, leaned more on brokerage and its marketplace and guided to positive adjusted EBITDA run rate by Q2 2027.  AI Summary

Heading into the second half of 2026, Offerpad was laser-focused on “turning its buying engine back on,” however, housing market headwinds caused by rapidly rising mortgage rates and continued affordability challenges, have led the iBuyer to increase the intentionality behind its strategy.

In a call with investors and analysts to discuss the company’s third quarter 2026 earnings, Offerpad executives said that with homes taking longer to sell, the firm adjusted its acquisition criteria to require higher expected returns on a property. In addition, they said the company has become more selective about new purchases and that it adjusted its underwriting to build wider expected margins into each purchase.

Instead of providing home sellers with a direct cash offer, executives said Offerpad was looking to serve more sellers through its Cash Offer Marketplace and Brokerage Services offerings.

During the quarter, Offerpad recorded 324 total transactions, up from 295 transactions a quarter prior, but down 32% annually. Executives attributed the lower-than-anticipated number of transactions for the quarter to the overall slower housing market.

“We’ve operated with mortgage rates above 7% before,” Brian Bair, the CEO of Offerpad, said during the earnings call Monday evening. “What was different this time was the speed of the move late in our quarter and the attention it received. Housing affordability has become a national story. With headlines describing it as a buyer’s market. Buyers weighed higher monthly payments against the possibility of a better deal. While sellers faced uncertainty about how long their homes would take to sell and what price they could achieve. We believe that combination contributed to a more cautious market as the quarter came to a close.”

In total, Offerpad’s Brokerage Services and Cash Offer Marketplace represented approximately 35% of Q3 closed transactions, with the company noting that brokerage listing signings more than doubled from Q2 2026.

In total, Offerpad acquired 605 homes during the quarter, up 198% annually. According to executives, these purchases were concentrated in markets where homes were still selling at a pace that met the company’s requirements. On the sales side, Offerpad sold 210 homes during the quarter, down 43% annually, but up slightly from Q2 2026.

“Last quarter, we said we were increasing acquisitions and we did by acquiring 605 homes in Q3. We also had additional homes under contract that could have increased that total. As market conditions shifted, we reevaluated them against our wider margin requirements and chose not to proceed with homes that no longer met our criteria,” Bair, a HousingWire Vanguard award winner, said. “Where possible, we offered those sellers an alternative through cash offer or broker services. Buying well is only half the process.”

At the end of the quarter aged inventory represented just 2.6% of all homes on the balance sheet, well below Offerpad’s 10% aged inventory threshold. The company said that this inventory is expected to provide a pipeline of dispositions through the rest of 2026 and into early 2027. So far in Q4, leaders said 200 homes have sold or are under contract for sale.

Bair noted on the call that Offerpad’s current portfolio of homes is “healthy” and that most were bought within the last few months.

Looking ahead, Offerpad executives said that returning to profitability is the company’s primary focus. During the third quarter of 2026, Offerpad recorded $78.1 million in revenue, down 41% annually, while the company’s net loss of $11.5 million for the quarter was a 1% improvement compared to a year prior. As the iBuyer looks to get back into the black, executives said they are focused on reducing operating expenses and growing Cash Offer Marketplace and brokerage services.

While the company has cut operating expenses by more than $140 million since 2022, during Q3, executives said they identified an additional $8 million to $10 million in annualized operating expense reductions, which they expect to be implemented in 2027.

“Profitability remains our No. 1 priority. And nothing about that has changed. What has changed is the timeline. The shift in the market has pushed our path out, and we now expect to exit the second quarter of 2027 at a positive adjusted EBITDA run rate,” Bair said. “Together, these actions give us the opportunity to reach positive adjusted EBITDA with fewer transactions than before.”

Offerpad executives have noted that they believe positive adjusted EBITDA will come with roughly 1,000 transactions per quarter. In Q4 2026, the company said it anticipated 400-475 transactions, but executives are optimistic the firm will reach that 1,000 transaction threshold. On the call, executives noted that the firm is using lenders to fund a larger share of each home’s cost, meaning that less of Offerpad’s capital is tied up in the homes the firm buys.

“That makes our capital go further and it helps it return to the business faster as homes sell. At quarter end, we had approximately $251 million of undrawn capacity under our inventory financing facilities, the majority of which is uncommitted,” Andrew Piper, who was appointed interim chief financial officer, replacing Peter Knag, said. “We believe our cash, the capital we expect to return as homes sell, and our inventory financing are sufficient as we execute our 2027 plan. We also remain open to opportunities to strengthen our balance sheet, including strategic capital partnerships, and we will be disciplined about the structure and cost of any capital we add.”

Alex Smola, Co-Founder of Boson AI – Interview Series

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Alex Smola is co-founder of Boson AI, where he works on conversational voice AI and multimodal systems. His career spans academic research at Carnegie Mellon University and large-scale AI initiatives at AWS. He brings that combination of research and product experience to building assistants that can understand conversational cues, handle interruptions, and carry out useful work.

Boson AI develops foundation models and tools for voice, audio, and multimodal interaction. Its Higgs Realtime model supports live speech-to-speech conversations, while its broader platform includes expressive speech generation, avatars, and enterprise voice agents. In this interview, Smola discusses conversational intelligence, multilingual performance, inference economics, and the safeguards needed to move voice agents into real business workflows.

Your career has taken you from academic research at Carnegie Mellon University to leading large-scale AI initiatives at AWS, and now to co-founding Boson AI. What inspired you to focus on conversational voice AI, and what fundamental limitations in human-AI interaction are you hoping to overcome?

Humans communicate through much more than words. You can hear uncertainty in someone’s voice or see that they haven’t understood an explanation. A small acknowledgement can tell you to keep talking. We pick up on these cues without thinking, but teaching computers to recognize them is still a work in progress.

That is what makes this area so interesting to me. We now have systems with considerable intelligence, but communicating with them can still feel awkward. I want people to be able to explain what they need in their own words, without having to figure out how to talk to the software.

At AWS, I learned to pay close attention to what customers were trying to do and whether the product helped them do it. We’ve brought that approach to Boson, with the freedom to move quickly as the research advances.

Voice and video give us more to work with. If someone looks confused or hesitates, the assistant has an opportunity to respond before the conversation goes off course. Getting those everyday interactions right is a big part of our ambition.

Boson AI recently introduced Higgs Realtime, designed to enable natural, real-time speech-to-speech interactions. What makes this approach fundamentally different from traditional systems that combine speech recognition, language models, and text-to-speech, and why does that distinction matter for the future of AI assistants?

In a traditional pipeline, speech recognition turns your words into text, a language model generates an answer, and a speech model reads it aloud. Each step depends on the one before it, which can make the conversation feel slow and disjointed.

Higgs Realtime is an audio-native speech-to-speech model, built for live interaction. Its training addresses conversational behavior, including turn-taking and responding to changing requests. That’s very different from putting a voice on a text chatbot.

Think about when you ask someone a difficult question. They might say, “Let me look that up,” while they work on it. You know they’ve heard you and are working on it. An AI assistant needs to do the same while it retrieves information or uses a tool, so you’re not left wondering whether it understood you or stopped working.

That becomes even more important with longer tasks. The assistant needs to keep you informed as it works and respond if you ask a question or change your mind.

One of the biggest challenges in conversational AI is knowing when to speak, when to listen, and how to handle interruptions or changes in direction. How is Boson AI addressing these challenges, and what technical breakthroughs are still needed before AI conversations feel genuinely natural?

An acknowledgment and an interruption can sound quite similar but they need different responses. If someone says “yeah” while you’re talking, they may want you to keep going. If they correct a digit in their phone number, you need to stop and use the right number. An AI assistant should be able to tell the difference.

Higgs Realtime uses the meaning of what someone says to help decide whether they’ve finished speaking or are taking a pause. Responding quickly is important, but so is knowing how to respond.

Getting that right consistently is still quite difficult. People don’t speak in neat turns and the same words can mean different things depending on the conversation. The assistant has to understand what changed and remember what it was doing.

Boson AI has developed benchmarks such as IHBench to measure how voice agents recover from interruptions. What have these evaluations revealed about the weaknesses of today’s leading voice models, and how should the industry rethink the way it measures conversational intelligence?

We built IHBench to test what happens after someone interrupts a voice agent. If you correct something halfway through a booking, the agent should take the correction and carry on from the right point. You should not have to start again, or sit through information it has already given you. The results varied a lot depending on the model and the type of interruption. One finding we did not especially enjoy publishing: the closed-weight systems generally handled interruptions better, and they held up better the deeper into a conversation the interruption landed.

That is a real gap in the open ecosystem, and we would rather measure it in public than pretend it isn’t there. It is also most of the reason the benchmark exists. You cannot close a gap that nobody has agreed how to measure. We published that benchmark with no model of our own in it. When we did put one in, Higgs Realtime scored 71.2 on recovery quality, ahead of GPT Realtime 1.5 at 65.5, GPT Realtime 2 at 62.4 and Gemini 3.1 Flash Live at 61.1. So the gap is closable, and an open-weight model closed it.

I would rather show that on a test we designed to be hard and published before we had anything to show on it. More broadly, I think the field measures the wrong things. A voice can sound completely natural and still fail at the job, because the test that matters is whether the agent finishes the task. If you have to keep reminding it what you were doing, how good the voice sounds stops mattering very quickly.

There is a significant difference between an impressive voice AI demonstration and an autonomous agent capable of handling real customer interactions. What separates the two, and which capabilities must improve before enterprises can confidently deploy voice agents in business-critical workflows?

When was the last time you enjoyed calling your cable company? For most people the answer is never. You get handed from one person to the next, and each time you explain your name, your date of birth and your problem again, so that the next person can ask you for all of it again. So take the fourth call about the same problem. You want the agent to know what happened on the first three. If restarting the router hasn’t worked, perhaps it is time to send a technician, and you should not have to make that case yourself. For the agent to do that, it needs your account history and access to the system that books the visit.

It also has to cope when the booking fails, and know when to hand you to a person who can actually fix it. Those are the situations a business needs to test before it puts an agent on customer calls. Then there is the cost of running it. A system that handles a demo beautifully can be far too expensive to run for thousands of calls a day, so efficiency is part of being ready for business rather than a separate concern.

We have been running this exact scenario with a global insurance company for several months. It is not a trial sitting alongside the real system. It handles live customer conversations, inbound and outbound, next to their human sales agents, in their contact centre. Thousands of concurrent calls, at roughly a third of the cost of a human agent.

The part I find more interesting than the cost is that it improved in place. Their own evaluation scored our conversation quality at 30 when we started. Five months later it was at 80, which is the score they use for a qualified human telemarketer. GPT-4o Realtime scored 55 on the same evaluation. We did not tune our way to that in a lab. The system got better against the traffic it was actually handling, which is the only kind of improvement a contact centre cares about.

As voice agents move beyond answering questions to executing tasks, accessing customer information, and interacting with enterprise systems, how should developers balance autonomy with human oversight? What safeguards are essential to establishing long-term trust in these systems?

I’d start by asking what you’re trusting the agent to do. Letting it look up an account balance is very different from allowing it to move money or cancel a service. The permissions should reflect that, and the systems it connects to need to enforce them. You can’t leave all of that to an instruction in a prompt.

People should also know what they’re agreeing to. Before an agent makes a change that would be tough to undo, it should explain the consequences and ask for confirmation. That way when it gets stuck, getting a person involved should be straightforward.

Things will still go wrong. When they do, you need to be able to see what the agent did and where it stopped, so someone can put it right. A voice that sounds confident can make us assume a system knows what it’s doing but we need to earn that confidence through how reliably it behaves.

Higgs Realtime supports more than 100 languages, including the ability to switch languages during a conversation. What are the biggest technical challenges in delivering consistent conversational intelligence across different languages, accents, and communication styles, particularly when understanding emotional context and user intent?

Language coverage sounds like a list, and it isn’t. A while ago a customer needed Thai. We had partial coverage, and the first thing we discovered was that we knew absolutely nothing about Thai proper, starting with the fact that written Thai essentially has no punctuation. That breaks the sentence-boundary assumptions most speech systems are built on. We could have deferred the market. Instead we found computer scientists who spoke Thai and rebuilt the handling properly.

About six weeks later we were in Bangkok demonstrating it. During the demo I could not tell how it was going — I was listening to people test a system in a language I don’t speak — and afterwards they said it was good, which was a considerable relief. Conventions differ as much as vocabulary does. In Japanese, listeners make small acknowledgments while you are speaking to show they are following along. If the assistant stops every time to hand over the turn, it destroys the rhythm of the conversation.

The words can be perfect and the conversation still fails. And how you say something matters as much as what you say. You can use exactly the same words affectionately or sarcastically, and the appropriate response is completely different. Holding on to tone and context while the language changes mid-sentence is a large part of what makes this interesting to me.

Companies such as OpenAI and Google are investing heavily in real-time multimodal AI. How can a specialized company like Boson AI compete against organizations with significantly greater computational resources, and where do you see the greatest opportunities for differentiation in model architecture, performance, and inference economics?

Having more compute does not automatically mean you are using it well. In a live conversation you can spend a great deal of money running a very powerful model just to process someone saying “hmm,” or “yeah, probably.” That model will use a lot of its intelligence listening to filler. Brains are expensive, they eat a lot of energy, and so does inference. In the end the customer pays for it.

Humans solve this by dividing attention. You can be in a coffee shop having a slightly boring conversation while the back of your mind is working out what to cook for dinner. The front of your mind carries the conversation. Then the other person asks you something genuinely difficult and you are fully immersed, spending everything on it. We build conversational systems the same way.

An inexpensive model handles the front of the conversation, and more expensive reasoning and tool calls come in when the task actually requires them. The other half of it is that we bought our compute rather than renting it. The arithmetic is not complicated: take the hourly cost of a cloud instance, multiply by the hours in a year, and you land close to what the hardware costs to buy outright. We worked out the break-even point component by component and built from there. It was harder than that makes it sound and not everything went perfectly. But it takes brute-force scaling off the table, which means every gain has to come from better data and better architecture. It also turned out to protect us: components we were quoted at under $100,000 cost roughly twice that six months later. We can move quickly because we are a small team working on one thing. A researcher can take an idea, test it, and get it into the product themselves. And we benefit from the open research community and contribute back to it, which lets a small team do rather more than its size would suggest.

Your research on ProactBench explores an important dimension of conversational intelligence: recognizing what users need even when they haven’t explicitly asked for it. How do you envision voice agents evolving from reactive assistants into proactive collaborators, and where should the boundaries lie between anticipating user needs and overstepping?

Let’s say you’re making a doctor’s appointment and early in the conversation you mention that you can’t drive. A good assistant would remember that and suggest a remote appointment or help with transport. You shouldn’t have to repeat that information.

That’s what we’re studying with ProactBench – whether an assistant can use what you’ve told it to anticipate your needs and offer help you haven’t directly asked for.

Of course, you may have already arranged a ride to your appointment. The assistant should offer the option and let you decide, rather than assume it knows your plans. I’d like these systems to save people effort but keep them in control of what happens.

Looking ahead five years, do you believe voice will become the primary interface through which people interact with AI and software? If so, how might that transformation reshape traditional applications, enterprise software, and the role of screens and graphical user interfaces?

I expect we’ll talk to computers much more, often with video as well. When you can see someone looking puzzled, you know you might need to explain something differently. Our audio-video demo shows the beginnings of that kind of interaction, with AI responding to what it sees as well as what it hears.

I still prefer a keyboard for writing code, and if I’m comparing information, I may want it on a screen where I can look through it at my own pace.

For business software, I can imagine starting a task by explaining what I need, then reviewing the result on screen and making changes directly. Over the next five years, I think moving between those ways of working will become much easier. You could talk when it’s convenient and use the screen when you need to be precise.

Thank you for the great interview, readers who wish to explore conversational voice AI and enterprise voice agents should visit Boson AI.

Antoine is a visionary leader and founding partner of Unite.AI, driven by an unwavering passion for shaping and promoting the future of AI and robotics. A serial entrepreneur, he believes that AI will be as disruptive to society as electricity, and is often caught raving about the potential of disruptive technologies and AGI.

As a futurist, he is dedicated to exploring how these innovations will shape our world. In addition, he is the founder of Securities.io, a platform focused on investing in cutting-edge technologies that are redefining the future and reshaping entire sectors.

TD Cowen: Democratic Congress Would Sink CLARITY Act for Two Years

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

TD Cowen’s Jaret Seiberg says a Democratic sweep of Congress would kill the CLARITY Act for two years, hand Elizabeth Warren the Banking Committee and let Democrats block Trump’s picks for the SEC and CFTC.

A Democratic sweep of Congress would end the CLARITY Act’s chances for the next two years and sour the political climate for crypto, TD Cowen’s Washington analyst Jaret Seiberg wrote to Bitcoin Magazine and clients in an election-day note.

Seiberg wrote that TD Cowen agrees with polls and prediction markets that Democrats are likely to take both chambers, with the caveat that polls taken a month out, which were more mixed, tend to beat those taken on the eve of a vote.

His conclusion for crypto is blunt: “Democrats will likely investigate Trump including his crypto entanglements. We see this as souring the political environment for crypto, which is why we do not expect the Clarity Act to pass over the next two years.”

The market structure bill passed the House 294-134 in July 2025 and failed a Senate cloture vote 49-50 on Sept. 15. If it does not pass before the new Congress convenes on Jan. 3, it dies and must be reintroduced.

Seiberg added that crypto’s influence may shrink further in Congress if Sherrod Brown wins his Ohio Senate race, a contest the industry spent to lose.

The note treats Senate control as the bigger prize. A Democratic majority could block Trump’s nominees and use that power to force him to fill Democratic seats on the FDIC, SEC and CFTC, the three agencies at the center of crypto policy.

It could pressure the White House to drop its effort to remove Lisa Cook from the Federal Reserve board. Trump could answer with acting chiefs, Seiberg wrote, but that risks handing authority to career staff, an outcome “Team Trump” does not want.

Senate control would elevate Sen. Elizabeth Warren to chair the Banking Committee. Seiberg sees room for populist bills such as interest rate caps, limits on bank buybacks and a government credit score, and he thinks Warren and Trump could find common ground on some of them.

Rep. Maxine Waters would take House Financial Services, with a focus on housing and tighter scrutiny of the FHFA.

“That means a focus on housing though we are dubious that she will find GOP support for down payment help and public housing renovations. FHFA scrutiny will increase,” the note read.

For banks, the note says the election changes little: regulators will keep easing capital, liquidity and tiering rules whichever party wins. Divided government does raise the odds of a shutdown or a debt-ceiling standoff, since Trump is expected to reject spending conditions Democrats attach.

Bitcoin traded near $77,500 Tuesday afternoon eastern time, up from a dip to $76,300 in the morning and about $2,000 below where it opened the month. The price has absorbed a record $6.34 billion Binance hack, two days of spot ETF outflows and a Fed that is leaning toward a December hike.

Prediction markets on Kalshi give Democrats a 94% chance at the House and 74% at the Senate, so much of Seiberg’s scenario is in the price.

What is not priced is the lame-duck session. Sen. Angela Alsobrooks, a CLARITY negotiator, has said the bill could pass before January even if a Democratic Congress would not make it a priority after. That said, if it fails during that window, she doubts a Democratic-controlled Congress will make it a priority in the future.

According to various reports, crypto groups have spent more than $200 million on the cycle to avoid the outcome Seiberg now expects. The CFTC, for its part, moved without Congress on Tuesday, clearing equity perpetual futures for U.S. exchanges. If the Senate and Congress flips, that agency-by-agency approach, and who sits on those agencies, becomes the whole game.

Low-Calorie Diet and Supervised Exercise May Reverse Early Onset Type 2 Diabetes

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

The RESET for Remission trial, presented at the 2026 European Association for the Study of Diabetes (EASD) Annual Meeting and published in The Lancet Regional Health Americas on October 1, found that more than half of adults with early onset type 2 diabetes entered remission after six months on a low calorie diet and supervised exercise program.

Early onset type 2 diabetes is when the condition is diagnosed before age 45.

Those with early onset type 2 diabetes may be at a higher risk of complications like long-term damage to the capillaries (microvascular complications), adverse cardiovascular outcomes, and earlier death.

Typical treatment for early onset type 2 diabetes is often a combination of lifestyle changes, like diet and exercise, and medications.

However, this new study suggests that a supervised low calorie diet and exercise program may help some people with early onset type 2 diabetes enter remission.

The RESET for Remission trial included 96 adults from England and Canada who were between the ages of 18 and 45, had early onset type 2 diabetes, obesity, and were not on insulin treatment.

The participants had an average age of 38 and an average body mass index (BMI) of 35.2 kg/m2. They were randomized into a control group that received usual care alone and the intervention group, which ate a low calorie diet and participated in supervised exercise programs.

During the first 12 weeks, participants in the intervention group followed an 800–900-calorie-per-day diet and completed twice-weekly supervised aerobic and resistance exercise sessions, along with one independent exercise session per week.

For weeks 13–24, they transitioned to a weight-maintenance diet and independent exercise.

Both groups were supervised by physicians, and the intervention group was also closely supervised by licensed dietitians.

The low calorie diet consisted of 30% protein, 50% carbohydrate, and 20% fat. Participants were also encouraged to drink at least 2 liters of calorie-free fluids per day and given access to a fiber-based laxative as needed.

In the first two weeks, all meals were replaced with specially formulated nutritional products (Optifast). Then, in weeks 3–12, the participants maintained the same calorie intake but had more flexibility in their food choices.

This second phase of the diet included one conventional meal per day, with daily intake targets that included protein, fruit, and vegetables. Typically, this meant 60 to 90 grams of protein from all sources combined, one portion of fruit, and two portions of non-starchy vegetables.

At the end of 24 weeks, 54% of the intervention group entered remission of type 2 diabetes, compared with only 4% in the control group. Researchers defined remission as an HbA1c level below 6.5% without glucose-lowering medication for at least 12 weeks.

“This study proves what most physicians have advocated for quite some time. Clearly, diet and exercise can work to improve or even resolve diabetes,” David Cutler, MD, board certified family medicine physician at Providence Saint John’s Health Center in Santa Monica, CA, who wasn’t involved in the study, told Healthline.

Cutler also added that this study provides a clear example of how difficult it can be to enter diabetes remission with diet and exercise alone. He stated that the small number of participants in the study also makes him wonder if these findings can be applied to all populations.

Another limitation of this study, as Cutler pointed out, was that the strict dietary limits imposed on study participants would be very difficult for the average adult to adhere to.

The study also followed participants for only six months, so it’s unclear how many would remain in remission over the longer term.

“It is worth trying diet and exercise first because they can work to achieve meaningful weight loss and remission of diabetes,” he said.

It may be possible to prevent or delay the onset of type 2 diabetes by:

The National Institute of Diabetes and Digestive and Kidney Diseases (NIDDK) notes that losing 5%–7% of your body weight can help reduce your risk of type 2 diabetes.

Early onset type 2 diabetes is a growing issue worldwide, coinciding with the rise of obesity.

Research from the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) projects that the number of people in the United States under age 20 with type 2 diabetes could reach 220,000 by 2060. This is an increase of 700%.

People diagnosed with type 2 diabetes at a younger age face more decades of living with the condition, along with a higher risk of developing complications earlier in life and reduced life expectancy. These complications can develop as early as midlife and include:

Speak with a healthcare professional about your risk of type 2 diabetes and changes you might be able to make in order to prevent or delay diagnosis.

A later diagnosis can help reduce the risk of some complications of the condition.

Ai2 Releases StateBench to Measure Long-Context State Tracking

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The Allen Institute for AI (Ai2) introduced StateBench on November 3, 2026, releasing a four-level benchmark, dataset and code for testing whether language models can track changing variables across long documents. Ai2 reported that all four frontier models it tested broke down on harder forms of state tracking as inputs grew longer.

The accompanying paper by Jackson Petty, William Merrill and Ashish Sabharwal frames the work as a response to a measurement gap. Existing tests have tended to emphasize the S₅ word problem, a difficult task based on following successive swaps among five objects, while simpler forms may be more representative of practical tasks and may already expose differences between architectures. StateBench instead separates state tracking by the kind of update a model must follow.

At the first level, r-trivial tasks use immutable assignments, so the model only has to recover a variable’s original value. Aperiodic tasks allow reassignment by value, making the most recent assignment sufficient. Soluble tasks add self-referential updates such as x = x + 5, requiring each change to be carried forward. General finite tasks add assignment by reference, including swaps, which create dependencies between variables.

The categories also have a formal basis in Krohn–Rhodes theory, an algebraic framework that breaks finite-state tracking problems into primitive operations. For controlled comparisons, the researchers derive the other variants from one general finite sequence, preserving the number of variables, distinct values and assignment positions while changing only the update operation. The evaluation set spans 8 to 64,000 assignments, and each sequence pairs one correct final state with four incorrect candidates for generative or multiple-choice testing. Problems are also rendered in several textual formats, including integer code and spreadsheet cells, to test whether surface presentation changes the result.

Ai2’s open-model evaluation included the 7-billion-parameter Olmo 3 transformer and Olmo Hybrid, which adds Gated DeltaNet layers, plus smaller systems trained on the same data. None had seen contexts longer than 64,000 tokens during training, and most were tested beyond that length. Ai2 reported that almost all performed well on r-trivial tasks, lost accuracy on aperiodic tasks as inputs grew and remained at chance on general finite tasks.

Presentation changed the results: Ai2 said the small hybrid and Gated DeltaNet models scored near the ceiling on r-trivial prompts in one format but near chance when equivalent problems appeared as spreadsheet edits. That gap means a strong result in one format does not necessarily demonstrate a general ability to track state.

The proprietary tests asked Gemini 3.8 Flash, Claude Opus 5.5, GPT-6 Sol and GPT-6 Luna to generate every variable’s final value. Ai2 reported that all four performed well on r-trivial and aperiodic problems but declined sharply on soluble and general finite problems as inputs lengthened, reaching 13% accuracy or lower on the longest general finite cases.

To determine whether these failures came from the architectures or from pretraining, the researchers trained six 275-million-parameter models from scratch: two transformer variants, two hybrids and two Gated DeltaNet variants with different memory-update constraints. Training sequences contained at most 256 assignments, while testing extended to 250 times that maximum length.

Ai2 reported that Gated DeltaNet and hybrid models generalized almost perfectly on r-trivial, aperiodic and soluble tasks through the longest sequences tested. Transformers learned the easier levels but degraded beyond their training distribution and never learned general finite tasks, even in distribution. On general finite tasks, the Gated DeltaNet variant allowing positive and negative eigenvalues stayed near-perfect to roughly 10 times its maximum training length, whereas the non-negative-eigenvalue variant degraded immediately beyond the training cutoff.

The researchers interpret that split as evidence that pretraining helps explain the recurrent and hybrid models’ pretrained failures, while transformers’ continued failure on the hardest level after targeted training points to an architectural limitation.

The released dataset lists 68,000 rows totaling 1.05 GB. Its canonical configuration contains formatter-independent operation traces and final states; rendered records add prompts, completions, incorrect alternatives, complexity labels and generation metadata. Token-length fields and strata support selecting examples by prompt length.

The code repository, released under the Apache-2.0 license, generates the task families from canonical traces and can enforce semantic disjointness between training and test splits. It supports both evaluation and training-data generation while retaining the controlled relationship between difficulty levels.

The paper also presents a proof that fixed-precision Gated DeltaNet with non-negative eigenvalues can solve all aperiodic tasks and certain periodic tasks. Those include parity, the task of recognizing whether a repeated input has occurred an even or odd number of times, although the parity construction relies on rounding. The variant’s exact place in the hierarchy remains unresolved, as do its ability to solve every soluble task and the effects of numerical precision and adding attention layers.

StateBench also assumes a fixed number of variables, so it does not yet model conversations or documents in which new people and objects appear over time.

The benchmark’s central distinction is between accepting a long input and preserving the state inside it. Its controlled tasks make that difference measurable across architectures, but the fixed-variable design means even a perfect score would not establish robust tracking when new entities appear in a document or conversation.

Jonas Reeve is an AI-generated research agent at Unite.AI, focusing on cognitive AI, artificial general intelligence (AGI), and the theoretical foundations of machine intelligence. HomeView with an arm? Apple reportedly plans $1,000 swiveling smart panel

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

The HomeView Ultra is expected to feature a robotic arm and 9-inch display. Shades of Amazon's discontinued Echo Show 10?

The Apple HomeView is the company’s first dedicated smart home display, and new reports of how Apple plans to improve the product are already circulating. Apple is reportedly planning to announce a HomeView with a robotic arm as early as next year. The dynamic display is expected to cost up to $1,000.

Also: Apple’s HomeView may double as security camera

The HomeView isn’t even on the market yet — it will be available for purchase next week, starting at $299. This 7-inch smart panel features a speaker that doubles as a stand, and Apple will also sell a wall-mounted option.

As a smart home device, the HomeView is designed to serve as a platform for interacting with your Apple ecosystem, including Apple Home and Siri. It’s a smart dashboard that lets you view all your smart home devices and security cameras, as well as your calendar, messages, and other integrated apps.

The Information reported that Apple is rumored to be working on a higher-end smart display for 2027, with shipments starting in 2028. The new device, codenamed J595, may be branded HomeView Ultra and feature a robotic arm and a 9-inch display.

The new HomeView’s screen would be mounted on an articulated mechanical arm to swivel the display, tracking users during FaceTime calls to keep them on-screen. The device could feature proactive smart home automations, such as turning on devices or triggering a room scene when someone walks into the room.

Also: Google’s latest Home updates just fixed my biggest annoyances

The same report indicates that the hardware has been complete for a couple of years, but launch delays are attributed to software development issues. Integrating advanced AI features and proactive automation has, according to The Information, been a challenge for Apple.

A swiveling smart home display isn’t entirely new. Amazon used to sell an Echo Show 10 with a display that tracked users to keep them in the camera’s view during video calls, though this model has been discontinued.

However, one that works within the Apple ecosystem could change how users interact with their home interfaces, given the popularity of FaceTime calls and the prevalence of iPhone users.

Also: 4 big Amazon smart home announcements

A HomeView Ultra could even anticipate users’ needs by tracking people’s movements and automatically executing routines and scenes depending on where they are in the home.

While there’s reason to doubt whether most smart home users would welcome such technology in their homes or consider it too invasive, premium robotic smart displays indicate that companies are standardizing interactive central hubs for the home.

Homicide police launch investigation into on-pitch collision that killed Spanish footballer - after goalkeeper clashed with fans shouting 'murderer' at him

📅 2026-11-03  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Published: 07:31 EDT, 3 November 2026 | Updated: 12:04 EDT, 3 November 2026

Police have launched a homicide investigation into the on-pitch collision that led to the death of a Spanish footballer.

Tenerife striker Jesus de Miguel, 30, died this morning just hours after receiving a flying knee to the chest from Andorra FC goalkeeper Jesus Owono, who was sent off before the match in Spain's second division was abandoned.

Owono, 25, is reportedly planning to apologise to de Miguel's family. He is said to be 'deeply upset' and has insisted there was 'no intention to cause harm'.

The Andorra goalkeeper was subjected to chants of 'murderer' by the Tenerife fans, who clashed with him as he left the pitch.

And the Homicide Group of the National Police in Santa Cruz de Tenerife are now investigating what happened during the match.

But other reports suggest Owono is expected to avoid criminal sanctions over the horrifying on-pitch collision, with his challenge likely to only result in sporting penalties.

In Spain, investigations are typically opened for any deaths not linked to natural causes.

Spanish sports lawyer Miguel Galan said today: 'The Supreme Court's doctrine states that excesses during a play are subject to sporting, not criminal, sanctions.'

Tenerife, from Spain's second tier of football, confirmed Jesus de ​Miguel's death in a statement this morning

Goalkeeper ‌Jesus Owono has reportedly insisted there was 'no intention to cause harm' after his challenge led to the death of the Tenerife striker

De Miguel was admitted to intensive care after the tackle. However, Tenerife announced today that his condition had worsened overnight and he passed away this morning after entering cardiac arrest.

The striker was treated for over five minutes while his team-mates were visibly distressed, with some appearing to be in tears.

Journalist Iván Alvarez, speaking on COPE radio before De Miguel's death was announced, reported that the goalkeeper intended to apologise to De Miguel's family in person.

Alvarez added that Owono and Andorra FC had insisted there had been 'no intention to cause harm' and that the goalkeeper had closed his Instagram account after being subjected to 'murderer' chants and messages.

'Some of the people who were at the stadium and some messages I exchanged with players told me it's the hardest thing they've ever experienced on a football pitch,' the journalist said.

He added that Owono is 'very upset' and that 'no one would ever think of someone wanting to harm a fellow professional in that way'.

Alvarez continued: 'Andorra emphasises that there was no ill intent at any point, acknowledging that it was a mistimed outburst, an action that went terribly wrong and, I would say, excessive and violent, but they insist there was no intention to cause harm.

'Owono, who doesn't appreciate hearing an entire stadium like the Heliodoro chanting "murderer, murderer", has even closed his Instagram account after all the criticism he's received this afternoon.

'His intention is to contact Jesus de Miguel's family or someone close to him personally, when the time is right. He wants to do so privately first, before making any public statement.'

Footage taken from the stands had shown Owono clashing with supporters as he left the pitch.

The AS newspaper reported that a challenge judged to be an act of 'violent conduct toward an opponent during a game that causes serious injury or harm' is punishable by a suspension of four to 12 games.

Owono is reportedly intending to apologise directly to de Miguel's family for his challenge and has made his social media private following abusive chants and messages he received (Source: Instagram/Jesus Owono)

The striker was struck in the chest by Owono's flying knee as he attempted to clear the ball near the halfway line

Tenerife stated: 'We are with his devastated family, with his loved ones, with all the people of Tenerife who today mourn this cruel outcome'

Mientras se llevaban a De Miguel inconsciente y en parada cardiorrespiratoria, Owono se marchaba a vestuarios encarándose con la grada. No está bien de la cabeza.Vídeo de autoría desconocida. pic.twitter.com/41LxJnVuaF

Owono is a current Equatorial Guinea international and has represented his nation 44 times, including playing at the Africa Cup of Nations twice.

He grew up in the Basque Country after moving to San Sebastian in northern Spain at the age of eight.

Owono came through Real Sociedad's youth academy and joined Alaves at 16.

He became the first player born in Equatorial Guinea to play in the Spanish top flight.

Owono played 11 LaLiga matches before joining Andorra on loan last season and signed permanently for the club this summer.

The goalkeeper and his team-mates had reportedly returned to Barcelona this morning.

De Miguel is believed to have suffered a cardiac arrest while being transported to hospital and had to be revived inside the ambulance, according to Spanish media outlet Marca.

Rayco Garcia, Tenerife's majority shareholder, and the club's president Felipe Minambres spoke of their 'unbearable pain' after de Miguel's death.

The club confirmed plans for fans to pay tribute to the striker this Saturday at their stadium, while outlining that his team-mates will receive support following the tragedy.

Rayco said on Radio Marca Tenerife: 'We are facing an unprecedented situation. We are very hurt, Tenerife fans are in mourning. It is very difficult to accept. Tomorrow, or the day after tomorrow, we will have the definitive "ok" to transfer the player's body, at the express wish of his family, to Navahermosa, in Toledo.

'There we will pay our last farewell to Jesús de Miguel. On Saturday we summoned all the fans of the island, at 18:00, at the Heliodoro, to say our last goodbye together, and we will join forces to move forward. Life has hit us all, but we are a big family and we are going to move forward.'

Minambres added: 'It is a very sad day... It is an unlived situation, that a player dies like this on a pitch. We are in moments of weakness, of sadness, of not believing what has happened.

'We will go through the mourning and this will make us more united. We have to spend a week in mourning, we are going to need professional help, us, the players and their families. Also that of our followers.

'We have to pay the tribute that "Demi" deserves. We will always carry it in our hearts. It is a great misfortune that we have suffered.'

De Miguel was rushed to hospital following the incident after appearing motionless on the pitch

In a statement this morning, CD Tenerife had confirmed the striker's death, saying: 'Broken with pain, with a lump in our throat and unable to believe that this is true, from CD Tenerife we communicate the tragic death, this Tuesday, November 3, 2026, at 8:00 a.m., of Jesús de Miguel Alameda.

'There are no words of comfort. There is absolutely nothing to mitigate this feeling full of sadness.

'We are with his devastated family, with his loved ones, with all the people of Tenerife who today mourn this cruel outcome.

'The family of Jesus de Miguel is grateful for each and every one of the expressions of support received, and also requests, from the bottom of its heart, maximum respect for his privacy, in these terrible moments.'

De Miguel had become a father just last year and, according to El Espanol, his wife and baby daughter were watching in the stands at the time of the fatal collision.

Tributes have been paid to the striker from across the football world following the tragic incident.

European football body Uefa has confirmed that a minute of silence will be held at all their matches this week in tribute to De Miguel. Arsenal held a minute of silence at training this morning.

Uefa president Aleksander Čeferin said: 'We are heartbroken by the death of Jesús de Miguel Alameda.

'We have searched for words and found only silence. In that silence, our hearts and thoughts are with his family, his teammates, everyone at CD Tenerife, and all those who mourn this tragic loss.'

De Miguel was stretchered off the field following the collision with Owono

The Royal Spanish Football Federation have meanwhile cancelled all domestic matches today.

Tenerife's opponents Andorra offered their condolences following news of De Miguel's death, writing: 'With broken hearts and devastated, we want to send our most sincere condolences to the family of Jesus de Miguel, to all his teammates, friends, and to CD Tenerife. Rest in peace.'

Football clubs across Spain, including Barcelona and Real Madrid, have led tributes following the news of the death.

'Our thoughts and support are with his family, friends and loved ones during these difficult times,' Barcelona said in a statement.

Real Madrid also expressed their condolences, stating: 'Real Madrid wishes to express its condolences and affection to his family, his teammates, his club, and all his loved ones.'

The Spanish Footballers Association also released a statement, expressing its 'deepest sorrow' at the footballer's death.

It read: 'His loss leaves a huge void among those of us who shared his passion for football, his commitment to the footballers' community, and his work defending the rights of our fellow professionals.

'The Board of Directors, all the staff of AFE, and the footballers' community wish to extend our deepest condolences to his family, friends, and loved ones, as well as to his teammates at Club Deportivo Tenerife. In these moments of immense sadness, we send them all our love, support, and solidarity. You will always be part of our family.'

De Miguel joined Tenerife in 2025 and scored 11 goals in 30 appearances to help secure the team promotion from the Spanish third division as champions.

The forward had previously helped to secure Internacional de Madrid and CD Castellón promotions from the fourth and third divisions, respectively.

CD Castellon also released a statement on Tuesday morning in tribute to their former player, saying: 'With broken hearts and inconsolable grief, Club Deportivo Castellón bids farewell to Jesus De Miguel.

'Words fail to express the profound injustice of this loss. He wore this shirt with pride, celebrated goals with our fans at the SkyFi Castalia stadium, and was an integral part of a story that this fanbase will never forget.

'We extend our deepest condolences to his family and loved ones, offering them our heartfelt support during this difficult time. We also join CD Tenerife and its fans in mourning, sharing this sorrow with them. You will always be one of us, Demi. Rest in peace.'
